A little, yes. I mean, I’m still showing up to clinic and doing the important parts of the job, so I don’t want to overstate it.

And outside of work, I’ve been avoiding social plans more. I tell myself I’m just tired, but it’s also that I don’t really trust my judgment or my energy right now. So… yes, I guess it is starting to affect how I function, even if I’m trying to keep it contained.

I replay little clinical imperfections or moments where I think, “I should’ve done that better,” and then it sort of turns into this feeling that maybe I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. Like those small things prove something worse about me as a doctor. I realize that probably sounds disproportionate… but it’s hard to let go of once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make these repetitive thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing I have to do, exactly. It’s more that I try to think it through or reassure myself that it wasn’t as bad as I’m making it out to be.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s what you mean, though. It doesn’t feel like a ritual or anything like that—just… trying to get my mind to settle, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your clinical imperfections interfere with your daily life or your ability to function as a doctor?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, I’m still showing up to clinic and doing the important parts of the job, so I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut I have noticed I’m not as reliable with some of the non-urgent administrative things, and I’ve had to ask for help or push things off more than I’d like. That’s embarrassing to say.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been avoiding social plans more. I tell myself I’m just tired, but it’s also that I don’t really trust my judgment or my energy right now. So… yes, I guess it is starting to affect how I function, even if I’m trying to keep it contained.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or behaviors in response to these thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of having to repeat an action or do something a certain number of times.\n\nI might look back over something in my head, or feel like I should double-check my reasoning, but I don’t have an overwhelming urge to perform a behavior to make it stop. At least, I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say anxious most of the time. I do get worried or tense, especially when I’m thinking about work or whether I’ve handled something correctly, but it’s not like constant panic or anything.\n\nMaybe it’s more… uneasy than anxious? I don’t know if that distinction makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to go back and check something you've already done to make sure it's correct?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not in a ritualized way, I don’t think… more because I don’t feel confident that I actually processed it the first time.\n\nLike, I’ll read the same lab result or an inbox message several times and still have this uncomfortable feeling that I’m not sure what I just read. So then even a simple admin decision can take me longer than it should, which is… frustrating and honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as “checking” in the way you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess... I’m here because I’ve been having a hard time with how I see myself at work. I’m a physician, and people trust me, but lately I keep feeling like I don’t really deserve that trust.\n\nIt sounds dramatic when I say it, and I don’t want to overstate it, but I keep replaying small things—like minor clinical imperfections—and treating them in my head like they mean I’m unsafe or dangerous as a doctor. I know that may sound excessive, but it’s been weighing on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad most of the time, exactly. Maybe… lower than usual, or just kind of weighed down. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But yes, I haven’t felt like myself lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call them “repetitive thoughts” in a severe way, but I do keep going over the same things.\n\nMostly it’s work-related. I replay little clinical imperfections or moments where I think, “I should’ve done that better,” and then it sort of turns into this feeling that maybe I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. Like those small things prove something worse about me as a doctor. I realize that probably sounds disproportionate… but it’s hard to let go of once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make these repetitive thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing I have to do, exactly. It’s more that I try to think it through or reassure myself that it wasn’t as bad as I’m making it out to be.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s what you mean, though. It doesn’t feel like a ritual or anything like that—just… trying to get my mind to settle, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your clinical imperfections interfere with your daily life or your ability to function as a doctor?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. That’s embarrassing to say.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been avoiding social plans more. I tell myself I’m just tired, but it’s also that I don’t really trust my judgment or my energy right now.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I think they do, yes. I mean, I don’t want to be the kind of doctor who’s obsessed with tiny flaws or who makes everything about himself.

But lately it’s hard to separate that from this feeling that maybe those small imperfections mean something bigger about me. Like… I don’t deserve the trust people put in me, and I keep replaying minor clinical things as if they’re evidence that I’m dangerous, or not safe to be doing this.

I know that sounds dramatic. I’m not trying to make excuses. It just feels very convincing in the moment, even though part of me knows that’s not how I’d judge someone else. Does that make sense?

I’m a physician, and people trust me, but lately I keep feeling like I don’t really deserve that trust.\n\nIt sounds dramatic when I say it, and I don’t want to overstate it, but I keep replaying small things—like minor clinical imperfections—and treating them in my head like they mean I’m unsafe or dangerous as a doctor. I know that may sound excessive, but it’s been weighing on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad most of the time, exactly. Maybe… lower than usual, or just kind of weighed down. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But yes, I haven’t felt like myself lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call them “repetitive thoughts” in a severe way, but I do keep going over the same things.\n\nMostly it’s work-related. I replay little clinical imperfections or moments where I think, “I should’ve done that better,” and then it sort of turns into this feeling that maybe I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. Like those small things prove something worse about me as a doctor. I realize that probably sounds disproportionate… but it’s hard to let go of once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make these repetitive thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing I have to do, exactly. It’s more that I try to think it through or reassure myself that it wasn’t as bad as I’m making it out to be.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s what you mean, though. I know that may sound excessive, but it’s been weighing on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad most of the time, exactly. Maybe… lower than usual, or just kind of weighed down. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But yes, I haven’t felt like myself lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call them “repetitive thoughts” in a severe way, but I do keep going over the same things.\n\nMostly it’s work-related. I replay little clinical imperfections or moments where I think, “I should’ve done that better,” and then it sort of turns into this feeling that maybe I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. Like those small things prove something worse about me as a doctor. I realize that probably sounds disproportionate… but it’s hard to let go of once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make these repetitive thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing I have to do, exactly. It’s more that I try to think it through or reassure myself that it wasn’t as bad as I’m making it out to be.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s what you mean, though. It doesn’t feel like a ritual or anything like that—just… trying to get my mind to settle, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your clinical imperfections interfere with your daily life or your ability to function as a doctor?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, I’m still showing up to clinic and doing the important parts of the job, so I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut I have noticed I’m not as reliable with some of the non-urgent administrative things, and I’ve had to ask for help or push things off more than I’d like. That’s embarrassing to say.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been avoiding social plans more. I tell myself I’m just tired, but it’s also that I don’t really trust my judgment or my energy right now. So… yes, I guess it is starting to affect how I function, even if I’m trying to keep it contained.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or behaviors in response to these thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of having to repeat an action or do something a certain number of times.\n\nI might look back over something in my head, or feel like I should double-check my reasoning, but I don’t have an overwhelming urge to perform a behavior to make it stop. At least, I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say anxious most of the time. I do get worried or tense, especially when I’m thinking about work or whether I’ve handled something correctly, but it’s not like constant panic or anything.\n\nMaybe it’s more… uneasy than anxious? I don’t know if that distinction makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to go back and check something you've already done to make sure it's correct?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not in a ritualized way, I don’t think… more because I don’t feel confident that I actually processed it the first time.\n\nLike, I’ll read the same lab result or an inbox message several times and still have this uncomfortable feeling that I’m not sure what I just read. So then even a simple admin decision can take me longer than it should, which is… frustrating and honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as “checking” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to mentally replay or reanalyze things in a specific way to try to get rid of these uncomfortable feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s in a specific way, no. I mean, I do turn things over in my head sometimes, but it’s not like I have a set mental sequence I have to follow to make the feeling go away.\n\nIt’s more just… I’m trying to reassure myself I didn’t miss something. But I wouldn’t say there’s a particular pattern to it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about these clinical imperfections in a particular way to try to make the uncomfortable feelings go away?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least I don’t think so. I might think about something more than I should, but it’s not like I have a particular way I have to analyze an imperfection to make the discomfort stop.\n\nI’m more just trying to decide whether it actually matters or whether I’m being too sensitive about it. I don’t know if that’s the distinction you’re getting at.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about clinical imperfections are ego-dystonic, meaning they go against your own beliefs or values as a doctor?\n\nPatient: I… I think they do, yes. I mean, I don’t want to be the kind of doctor who’s obsessed with tiny flaws or who makes everything about himself.\n\nBut lately it’s hard to separate that from this feeling that maybe those small imperfections mean something bigger about me. Like… I don’t deserve the trust people put in me, and I keep replaying minor clinical things as if they’re evidence that I’m dangerous, or not safe to be doing this.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic. I’m not trying to make excuses. It just feels very convincing in the moment, even though part of me knows that’s not how I’d judge someone else. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I’ve still been functioning, so I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s been more than a couple of weeks.\n\nFor months now, things I used to genuinely look forward to just feel… kind of flat. I used to plan weekend hikes, take sunrise photos, make these big Indonesian dinners for friends. And now even those feel like obligations, or like something I should care about but can’t quite make myself care about.\n\nI’m sorry if that sounds vague. I think that’s probably what you mean by losing interest?\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about clinical imperfections feel like they're invading your mind without you wanting to think about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as “invading,” exactly. They do come up when I don’t really want them to, but I can usually tell myself I’m just thinking through whether something mattered clinically.\n\nIt’s more like they stick around longer than I’d like, if that makes sense. I’m not trying to sit there and dwell on them, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound more severe than it is. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

But yes, I haven’t felt like myself lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call them “repetitive thoughts” in a severe way, but I do keep going over the same things.\n\nMostly it’s work-related. I replay little clinical imperfections or moments where I think, “I should’ve done that better,” and then it sort of turns into this feeling that maybe I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. Like those small things prove something worse about me as a doctor. I realize that probably sounds disproportionate… but it’s hard to let go of once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make these repetitive thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing I have to do, exactly. It’s more that I try to think it through or reassure myself that it wasn’t as bad as I’m making it out to be.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s what you mean, though. It doesn’t feel like a ritual or anything like that—just… trying to get my mind to settle, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your clinical imperfections interfere with your daily life or your ability to function as a doctor?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, I’m still showing up to clinic and doing the important parts of the job, so I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut I have noticed I’m not as reliable with some of the non-urgent administrative things, and I’ve had to ask for help or push things off more than I’d like. That’s embarrassing to say.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been avoiding social plans more. I tell myself I’m just tired, but it’s also that I don’t really trust my judgment or my energy right now. So… yes, I guess it is starting to affect how I function, even if I’m trying to keep it contained.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or behaviors in response to these thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of having to repeat an action or do something a certain number of times.\n\nI might look back over something in my head, or feel like I should double-check my reasoning, but I don’t have an overwhelming urge to perform a behavior to make it stop. At least, I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say anxious most of the time. I do get worried or tense, especially when I’m thinking about work or whether I’ve handled something correctly, but it’s not like constant panic or anything.\n\nMaybe it’s more… uneasy than anxious? I don’t know if that distinction makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to go back and check something you've already done to make sure it's correct?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not in a ritualized way, I don’t think… more because I don’t feel confident that I actually processed it the first time.\n\nLike, I’ll read the same lab result or an inbox message several times and still have this uncomfortable feeling that I’m not sure what I just read. So then even a simple admin decision can take me longer than it should, which is… frustrating and honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as “checking” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to mentally replay or reanalyze things in a specific way to try to get rid of these uncomfortable feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s in a specific way, no. I mean, I do turn things over in my head sometimes, but it’s not like I have a set mental sequence I have to follow to make the feeling go away.\n\nIt’s more just… I’m trying to reassure myself I didn’t miss something. But I wouldn’t say there’s a particular pattern to it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about these clinical imperfections in a particular way to try to make the uncomfortable feelings go away?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least I don’t think so. I might think about something more than I should, but it’s not like I have a particular way I have to analyze an imperfection to make the discomfort stop.\n\nI’m more just trying to decide whether it actually matters or whether I’m being too sensitive about it. I don’t know if that’s the distinction you’re getting at.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about clinical imperfections are ego-dystonic, meaning they go against your own beliefs or values as a doctor?\n\nPatient: I… I think they do, yes. I mean, I don’t want to be the kind of doctor who’s obsessed with tiny flaws or who makes everything about himself.\n\nBut lately it’s hard to separate that from this feeling that maybe those small imperfections mean something bigger about me. Like… I don’t deserve the trust people put in me, and I keep replaying minor clinical things as if they’re evidence that I’m dangerous, or not safe to be doing this.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic. I’m not trying to make excuses. It just feels very convincing in the moment, even though part of me knows that’s not how I’d judge someone else. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I’ve still been functioning, so I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s been more than a couple of weeks.\n\nFor months now, things I used to genuinely look forward to just feel… kind of flat. I used to plan weekend hikes, take sunrise photos, make these big Indonesian dinners for friends. And now even those feel like obligations, or like something I should care about but can’t quite make myself care about.\n\nI’m sorry if that sounds vague. I think that’s probably what you mean by losing interest?\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about clinical imperfections feel like they're invading your mind without you wanting to think about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as “invading,” exactly. They do come up when I don’t really want them to, but I can usually tell myself I’m just thinking through whether something mattered clinically.\n\nIt’s more like they stick around longer than I’d like, if that makes sense. I’m not trying to sit there and dwell on them, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound more severe than it is. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform any specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of a ritual, at least.\n\nI mean, I might double-check something or mentally review what happened, but I’d like to think that’s just being careful, not some kind of compulsion. I’m not doing a specific sequence or anything like that to make the anxiety go away.\n\nUnless you mean something broader by “rituals”? I don’t want to miss what you’re asking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Maybe… lower than usual, or just kind of weighed down. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But yes, I haven’t felt like myself lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call them “repetitive thoughts” in a severe way, but I do keep going over the same things.\n\nMostly it’s work-related. I replay little clinical imperfections or moments where I think, “I should’ve done that better,” and then it sort of turns into this feeling that maybe I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. Like those small things prove something worse about me as a doctor. I realize that probably sounds disproportionate… but it’s hard to let go of once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make these repetitive thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing I have to do, exactly. It’s more that I try to think it through or reassure myself that it wasn’t as bad as I’m making it out to be.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s what you mean, though. It doesn’t feel like a ritual or anything like that—just… trying to get my mind to settle, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your clinical imperfections interfere with your daily life or your ability to function as a doctor?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, I’m still showing up to clinic and doing the important parts of the job, so I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut I have noticed I’m not as reliable with some of the non-urgent administrative things, and I’ve had to ask for help or push things off more than I’d like. That’s embarrassing to say.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been avoiding social plans more. I tell myself I’m just tired, but it’s also that I don’t really trust my judgment or my energy right now. So… yes, I guess it is starting to affect how I function, even if I’m trying to keep it contained.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or behaviors in response to these thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of having to repeat an action or do something a certain number of times.\n\nI might look back over something in my head, or feel like I should double-check my reasoning, but I don’t have an overwhelming urge to perform a behavior to make it stop. At least, I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say anxious most of the time. I do get worried or tense, especially when I’m thinking about work or whether I’ve handled something correctly, but it’s not like constant panic or anything.\n\nMaybe it’s more… uneasy than anxious? I don’t know if that distinction makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to go back and check something you've already done to make sure it's correct?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not in a ritualized way, I don’t think… more because I don’t feel confident that I actually processed it the first time.\n\nLike, I’ll read the same lab result or an inbox message several times and still have this uncomfortable feeling that I’m not sure what I just read. So then even a simple admin decision can take me longer than it should, which is… frustrating and honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as “checking” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to mentally replay or reanalyze things in a specific way to try to get rid of these uncomfortable feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s in a specific way, no. I mean, I do turn things over in my head sometimes, but it’s not like I have a set mental sequence I have to follow to make the feeling go away.\n\nIt’s more just… I’m trying to reassure myself I didn’t miss something. But I wouldn’t say there’s a particular pattern to it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about these clinical imperfections in a particular way to try to make the uncomfortable feelings go away?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least I don’t think so. I might think about something more than I should, but it’s not like I have a particular way I have to analyze an imperfection to make the discomfort stop.\n\nI’m more just trying to decide whether it actually matters or whether I’m being too sensitive about it. I don’t know if that’s the distinction you’re getting at.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about clinical imperfections are ego-dystonic, meaning they go against your own beliefs or values as a doctor?\n\nPatient: I… I think they do, yes. I mean, I don’t want to be the kind of doctor who’s obsessed with tiny flaws or who makes everything about himself.\n\nBut lately it’s hard to separate that from this feeling that maybe those small imperfections mean something bigger about me. Like… I don’t deserve the trust people put in me, and I keep replaying minor clinical things as if they’re evidence that I’m dangerous, or not safe to be doing this.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic. I’m not trying to make excuses. It just feels very convincing in the moment, even though part of me knows that’s not how I’d judge someone else. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I’ve still been functioning, so I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s been more than a couple of weeks.\n\nFor months now, things I used to genuinely look forward to just feel… kind of flat. I used to plan weekend hikes, take sunrise photos, make these big Indonesian dinners for friends. And now even those feel like obligations, or like something I should care about but can’t quite make myself care about.\n\nI’m sorry if that sounds vague. I think that’s probably what you mean by losing interest?\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about clinical imperfections feel like they're invading your mind without you wanting to think about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as “invading,” exactly. They do come up when I don’t really want them to, but I can usually tell myself I’m just thinking through whether something mattered clinically.\n\nIt’s more like they stick around longer than I’d like, if that makes sense. I’m not trying to sit there and dwell on them, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound more severe than it is. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform any specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of a ritual, at least.\n\nI mean, I might double-check something or mentally review what happened, but I’d like to think that’s just being careful, not some kind of compulsion. I’m not doing a specific sequence or anything like that to make the anxiety go away.\n\nUnless you mean something broader by “rituals”? I don’t want to miss what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are specific actions you must take when you have these thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least I don’t think there’s anything I *must* do.\n\nSometimes I’ll go back over it in my head or check something if it seems reasonable, but it doesn’t feel like I have a required action, if that makes sense. I’m not trying to dodge the question—I just don’t want to label normal caution as something more than it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F32.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess... I’m here because I’ve been having a hard time with how I see myself at work. I’m a physician, and people trust me, but lately I keep feeling like I don’t really deserve that trust.\n\nIt sounds dramatic when I say it, and I don’t want to overstate it, but I keep replaying small things—like minor clinical imperfections—and treating them in my head like they mean I’m unsafe or dangerous as a doctor. I know that may sound excessive, but it’s been weighing on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad most of the time, exactly. Maybe… lower than usual, or just kind of weighed down. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But yes, I haven’t felt like myself lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if I’d call them “repetitive thoughts” in a severe way, but I do keep going over the same things.\n\nMostly it’s work-related. I replay little clinical imperfections or moments where I think, “I should’ve done that better,” and then it sort of turns into this feeling that maybe I don’t deserve the trust people put in me. Like those small things prove something worse about me as a doctor. I realize that probably sounds disproportionate… but it’s hard to let go of once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make these repetitive thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing I have to do, exactly. It’s more that I try to think it through or reassure myself that it wasn’t as bad as I’m making it out to be.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s what you mean, though. It doesn’t feel like a ritual or anything like that—just… trying to get my mind to settle, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your clinical imperfections interfere with your daily life or your ability to function as a doctor?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, I’m still showing up to clinic and doing the important parts of the job, so I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut I have noticed I’m not as reliable with some of the non-urgent administrative things, and I’ve had to ask for help or push things off more than I’d like. That’s embarrassing to say.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been avoiding social plans more. I tell myself I’m just tired, but it’s also that I don’t really trust my judgment or my energy right now. So… yes, I guess it is starting to affect how I function, even if I’m trying to keep it contained.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or behaviors in response to these thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of having to repeat an action or do something a certain number of times.\n\nI might look back over something in my head, or feel like I should double-check my reasoning, but I don’t have an overwhelming urge to perform a behavior to make it stop. At least, I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say anxious most of the time. I do get worried or tense, especially when I’m thinking about work or whether I’ve handled something correctly, but it’s not like constant panic or anything.\n\nMaybe it’s more… uneasy than anxious? I don’t know if that distinction makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to go back and check something you've already done to make sure it's correct?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not in a ritualized way, I don’t think… more because I don’t feel confident that I actually processed it the first time.\n\nLike, I’ll read the same lab result or an inbox message several times and still have this uncomfortable feeling that I’m not sure what I just read. So then even a simple admin decision can take me longer than it should, which is… frustrating and honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as “checking” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to mentally replay or reanalyze things in a specific way to try to get rid of these uncomfortable feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s in a specific way, no. I mean, I do turn things over in my head sometimes, but it’s not like I have a set mental sequence I have to follow to make the feeling go away.\n\nIt’s more just… I’m trying to reassure myself I didn’t miss something. But I wouldn’t say there’s a particular pattern to it. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about these clinical imperfections in a particular way to try to make the uncomfortable feelings go away?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least I don’t think so. I might think about something more than I should, but it’s not like I have a particular way I have to analyze an imperfection to make the discomfort stop.\n\nI’m more just trying to decide whether it actually matters or whether I’m being too sensitive about it. I don’t know if that’s the distinction you’re getting at.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about clinical imperfections are ego-dystonic, meaning they go against your own beliefs or values as a doctor?\n\nPatient: I… I think they do, yes. I mean, I don’t want to be the kind of doctor who’s obsessed with tiny flaws or who makes everything about himself.\n\nBut lately it’s hard to separate that from this feeling that maybe those small imperfections mean something bigger about me. Like… I don’t deserve the trust people put in me, and I keep replaying minor clinical things as if they’re evidence that I’m dangerous, or not safe to be doing this.\n\nI know that sounds dramatic. I’m not trying to make excuses. It just feels very convincing in the moment, even though part of me knows that’s not how I’d judge someone else. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I’ve still been functioning, so I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s been more than a couple of weeks.\n\nFor months now, things I used to genuinely look forward to just feel… kind of flat. I used to plan weekend hikes, take sunrise photos, make these big Indonesian dinners for friends. And now even those feel like obligations, or like something I should care about but can’t quite make myself care about.\n\nI’m sorry if that sounds vague. I think that’s probably what you mean by losing interest?\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about clinical imperfections feel like they're invading your mind without you wanting to think about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as “invading,” exactly. They do come up when I don’t really want them to, but I can usually tell myself I’m just thinking through whether something mattered clinically.\n\nIt’s more like they stick around longer than I’d like, if that makes sense. I’m not trying to sit there and dwell on them, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound more severe than it is. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform any specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of a ritual, at least.\n\nI mean, I might double-check something or mentally review what happened, but I’d like to think that’s just being careful, not some kind of compulsion. I’m not doing a specific sequence or anything like that to make the anxiety go away.\n\nUnless you mean something broader by “rituals”? I don’t want to miss what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are specific actions you must take when you have these thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least I don’t think there’s anything I *must* do.\n\nSometimes I’ll go back over it in my head or check something if it seems reasonable, but it doesn’t feel like I have a required action, if that makes sense. I’m not trying to dodge the question—I just don’t want to label normal caution as something more than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must perform a specific action when these thoughts about clinical imperfections come up?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I *must* perform a specific action, no.\n\nIt’s more that I might feel better if I’ve thought it through or checked that I didn’t miss something, but it doesn’t feel like a fixed thing I have to do every time. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say “compelled,” no… not in the sense that I feel forced to do something.

I might sort of pause and think it through, maybe reassure myself that I handled it reasonably, but I don’t have a specific thing I feel driven to do every time. If that’s the distinction you’re getting at, I think the answer is no.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess... I’m here because I’ve been having a hard time with how I see myself at work. I’m a physician, and people trust me, but lately I keep feeling like I don’t really deserve that trust.\n\nIt sounds dramatic when I say it, and I don’t want to overstate it, but I keep replaying small things—like minor clinical imperfections—and treating them in my head like they mean I’m unsafe or dangerous as a doctor. I know that may sound excessive, but it’s been weighing on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad most of the time, exactly. Maybe… lower than usual, or just kind of weighed down. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Like those small things prove something worse about me as a doctor. I realize that probably sounds disproportionate… but it’s hard to let go of once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make these repetitive thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing I have to do, exactly. It’s more that I try to think it through or reassure myself that it wasn’t as bad as I’m making it out to be.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s what you mean, though. It doesn’t feel like a ritual or anything like that—just… trying to get my mind to settle, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your clinical imperfections interfere with your daily life or your ability to function as a doctor?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, I’m still showing up to clinic and doing the important parts of the job, so I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut I have noticed I’m not as reliable with some of the non-urgent administrative things, and I’ve had to ask for help or push things off more than I’d like. That’s embarrassing to say.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been avoiding social plans more. I tell myself I’m just tired, but it’s also that I don’t really trust my judgment or my energy right now. So… yes, I guess it is starting to affect how I function, even if I’m trying to keep it contained.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or behaviors in response to these thoughts about clinical imperfections?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of having to repeat an action or do something a certain number of times.\n\nI might look back over something in my head, or feel like I should double-check my reasoning, but I don’t have an overwhelming urge to perform a behavior to make it stop. At least, I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say anxious most of the time. I do get worried or tense, especially when I’m thinking about work or whether I’ve handled something correctly, but it’s not like constant panic or anything.\n\nMaybe it’s more… uneasy than anxious? I don’t know if that distinction makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to go back and check something you've already done to make sure it's correct?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. Not in a ritualized way, I don’t think… more because I don’t feel confident that I actually processed it the first time.\n\nLike, I’ll read the same lab result or an inbox message several times and still have this uncomfortable feeling that I’m not sure what I just read. So then even a simple admin decision can take me longer than it should, which is… frustrating and honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as “checking” in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to mentally replay or reanalyze things in a specific way to try to get rid of these uncomfortable feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s in a specific way, no. I don’t know if that’s the distinction you’re getting at.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about clinical imperfections are ego-dystonic, meaning they go against your own beliefs or values as a doctor?\n\nPatient: I… I think they do, yes. I mean, I don’t want to be the kind of doctor who’s obsessed with tiny flaws or who makes everything about himself.\n\nBut lately it’s hard to separate that from this feeling that maybe those small imperfections mean something bigger about me. I’m not trying to make excuses. It just feels very convincing in the moment, even though part of me knows that’s not how I’d judge someone else. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think they do, at least sometimes.

I’m not sitting there trying to think badly of myself, but these little things from work will just come into my head, and then I start replaying them like they mean something bigger about me. I think… more about worthlessness, if I’m being honest.

I mean, yes, it starts with a fear that I might have made a mistake, but it doesn’t stay there. I end up replaying these small clinical imperfections like they’re evidence that I shouldn’t be trusted, or that I’m somehow a dangerous doctor.

I don’t know if that’s the distinction you’re getting at.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about clinical imperfections are ego-dystonic, meaning they go against your own beliefs or values as a doctor?\n\nPatient: I… I think they do, yes.
